But to me it feels like there are patterns—like the invitations I get, the topics at panels, or who ends up near me at events—and it feels like they’re connected to who I’m meeting with or what I’m doing. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be coincidence, I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these beliefs about being tracked?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months, I think. That’s when I started really noticing the feeling that people were tracking my client meetings and sending those kinds of signals.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief about being tracked affected your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’m still working, but not at the same level. My practice has gotten smaller because I’ll miss follow-ups or put off reaching back out to clients. I’ve also been avoiding some public panels or events, which used to be a normal part of staying connected. So I’m managing, but it’s definitely affected how much client contact I keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about being tracked led to any significant anxiety or fear for you?\n\nPatient: I mean… some, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but it does make me tense. Like if I’m going to an event or meeting people, I start worrying about whether it’s connected somehow, and that can make me avoid it or second-guess things. It’s more a constant uneasiness than panic.\n\nDoctor: When you think about these patterns and signals, do you ever question whether they might not be real, or do you feel pretty certain that they are?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes. I’ll tell myself, you know, maybe the invitations or the panel topics or where people are sitting are just coincidences. But then it keeps lining up in a way that feels too specific, like people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I’m meeting with. So… I can doubt it for a minute, but I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the idea that people in arts and finance circles are tracking you is something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it could happen. I mean, these are real circles here in Cincinnati, and people do talk and know each other. It doesn’t feel impossible to me that someone could be keeping track of who I’m meeting with, especially through invitations or panel topics or how things are arranged at events. I know some of it could be coincidence, but it feels too specific for me to just dismiss.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that people in arts and finance circles are actually tracking you?\n\nPatient: I’d say I’m pretty certain… not 100 percent, because I do have moments where I tell myself maybe it’s coincidence. But most of the time it feels real to me. The way invitations come in, the panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events—it feels like they’re quietly tracking who I’m meeting with. I have a hard time letting that go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the people tracking you as having a logical or understandable motive for doing so?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, it feels understandable to me. In those arts and finance circles, people know each other, and who I meet with could matter to them—clients, connections, that kind of thing. So if they were trying to keep track quietly, I can see a reason for it. I know it might sound like I’m reading into things, but when the invitations and panel topics and seating keep lining up, it feels like there’s a purpose behind it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the idea of people tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements is a belief that most people would consider unusual or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. Like, if I say out loud that invitations or panel topics or where people sit near me are signals, I know that sounds unusual. But to me it still feels connected, like it keeps lining up too specifically to ignore. Even when I try to tell myself it might just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't believe people were tracking you, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: There are moments where I question it, yeah. Like I’ll tell myself maybe the invitations or the seating stuff is just coincidence. But I don’t think there’s been a real stretch of time where I fully didn’t believe it. It keeps coming back to feeling connected, and I can’t really let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m still working, but not at the same level. My practice has gotten smaller because I’ll miss follow-ups or put off reaching back out to clients. I’ve also been avoiding some public panels or events, which used to be a normal part of staying connected. So I’m managing, but it’s definitely affected how much client contact I keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about being tracked led to any significant anxiety or fear for you?\n\nPatient: I mean… some, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but it does make me tense. Like if I’m going to an event or meeting people, I start worrying about whether it’s connected somehow, and that can make me avoid it or second-guess things. It’s more a constant uneasiness than panic.\n\nDoctor: When you think about these patterns and signals, do you ever question whether they might not be real, or do you feel pretty certain that they are?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes. I’ll tell myself, you know, maybe the invitations or the panel topics or where people are sitting are just coincidences. But then it keeps lining up in a way that feels too specific, like people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I’m meeting with. So… I can doubt it for a minute, but I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the idea that people in arts and finance circles are tracking you is something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it could happen. I mean, these are real circles here in Cincinnati, and people do talk and know each other. It doesn’t feel impossible to me that someone could be keeping track of who I’m meeting with, especially through invitations or panel topics or how things are arranged at events. I know some of it could be coincidence, but it feels too specific for me to just dismiss.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that people in arts and finance circles are actually tracking you?\n\nPatient: I’d say I’m pretty certain… not 100 percent, because I do have moments where I tell myself maybe it’s coincidence. But most of the time it feels real to me. The way invitations come in, the panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events—it feels like they’re quietly tracking who I’m meeting with. I have a hard time letting that go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the people tracking you as having a logical or understandable motive for doing so?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, it feels understandable to me. In those arts and finance circles, people know each other, and who I meet with could matter to them—clients, connections, that kind of thing. So if they were trying to keep track quietly, I can see a reason for it. I know it might sound like I’m reading into things, but when the invitations and panel topics and seating keep lining up, it feels like there’s a purpose behind it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the idea of people tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements is a belief that most people would consider unusual or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. Like, if I say out loud that invitations or panel topics or where people sit near me are signals, I know that sounds unusual. But to me it still feels connected, like it keeps lining up too specifically to ignore. Even when I try to tell myself it might just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't believe people were tracking you, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: There are moments where I question it, yeah. Like I’ll tell myself maybe the invitations or the seating stuff is just coincidence. But I don’t think there’s been a real stretch of time where I fully didn’t believe it. It keeps coming back to feeling connected, and I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the belief that people are tracking you through these specific methods as something that sounds normal and reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: To me, it does feel reasonable, yeah. I mean, I know if I explain it to someone else, it probably sounds odd. But in my head, the way certain invitations come up, or panel topics match things, or the same kinds of people end up near me at events… it feels too specific to just ignore. So I can tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, but I still can’t really shake the belief that they’re tracking me somehow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think I could explain the pattern to someone, like why the invitations or panel topics feel connected to who I’m meeting with. But whether they’d see it as logical… I’m not sure. I know it probably sounds like coincidence to other people.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this belief that certain people around the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are kind of tracking me—like who I’m meeting with for clients, where I’m going, things like that. I notice little signals in invitations, panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events. Part of me tells myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go, and it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these beliefs about being tracked as something that most people would find hard to believe or understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. I know it sounds unlikely when I say it out loud. But to me it feels like there are patterns—like the invitations I get, the topics at panels, or who ends up near me at events—and it feels like they’re connected to who I’m meeting with or what I’m doing. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be coincidence, I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these beliefs about being tracked?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months, I think. That’s when I started really noticing the feeling that people were tracking my client meetings and sending those kinds of signals.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief about being tracked affected your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’m still working, but not at the same level. My practice has gotten smaller because I’ll miss follow-ups or put off reaching back out to clients. I’ve also been avoiding some public panels or events, which used to be a normal part of staying connected. So I’m managing, but it’s definitely affected how much client contact I keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about being tracked led to any significant anxiety or fear for you?\n\nPatient: I mean… some, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but it does make me tense. Like if I’m going to an event or meeting people, I start worrying about whether it’s connected somehow, and that can make me avoid it or second-guess things. It’s more a constant uneasiness than panic.\n\nDoctor: When you think about these patterns and signals, do you ever question whether they might not be real, or do you feel pretty certain that they are?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes. I’ll tell myself, you know, maybe the invitations or the panel topics or where people are sitting are just coincidences. But then it keeps lining up in a way that feels too specific, like people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I’m meeting with. So… I can doubt it for a minute, but I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the idea that people in arts and finance circles are tracking you is something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it could happen. I mean, these are real circles here in Cincinnati, and people do talk and know each other. It doesn’t feel impossible to me that someone could be keeping track of who I’m meeting with, especially through invitations or panel topics or how things are arranged at events. I know some of it could be coincidence, but it feels too specific for me to just dismiss.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that people in arts and finance circles are actually tracking you?\n\nPatient: I’d say I’m pretty certain… not 100 percent, because I do have moments where I tell myself maybe it’s coincidence. But most of the time it feels real to me. The way invitations come in, the panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events—it feels like they’re quietly tracking who I’m meeting with. I have a hard time letting that go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the people tracking you as having a logical or understandable motive for doing so?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, it feels understandable to me. In those arts and finance circles, people know each other, and who I meet with could matter to them—clients, connections, that kind of thing. So if they were trying to keep track quietly, I can see a reason for it. I know it might sound like I’m reading into things, but when the invitations and panel topics and seating keep lining up, it feels like there’s a purpose behind it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the idea of people tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements is a belief that most people would consider unusual or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. Like, if I say out loud that invitations or panel topics or where people sit near me are signals, I know that sounds unusual. But to me it still feels connected, like it keeps lining up too specifically to ignore. Even when I try to tell myself it might just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't believe people were tracking you, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: There are moments where I question it, yeah. Like I’ll tell myself maybe the invitations or the seating stuff is just coincidence. But I don’t think there’s been a real stretch of time where I fully didn’t believe it. It keeps coming back to feeling connected, and I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the belief that people are tracking you through these specific methods as something that sounds normal and reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: To me, it does feel reasonable, yeah. I mean, I know if I explain it to someone else, it probably sounds odd. But in my head, the way certain invitations come up, or panel topics match things, or the same kinds of people end up near me at events… it feels too specific to just ignore. So I can tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, but I still can’t really shake the belief that they’re tracking me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your personal relationships due to this belief that people are tracking you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I still keep up with things, mostly, like work gets done in pieces. But I don’t have the same energy for people. I’ll let client follow-ups sit longer than I should, and outside of work I’ve been skipping jazz nights or pickup basketball.\n\nWhen friends ask what’s going on, I usually just give short answers. It’s not that I don’t care about them exactly… talking just feels like more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the signals you think you're receiving through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements as something that could be understood or explained by others in a logical way?\n\nPatient: I think I could explain the pattern to someone, like why the invitations or panel topics feel connected to who I’m meeting with. But whether they’d see it as logical… I’m not sure. I know it probably sounds like coincidence to other people.\n\nFor me, though, it feels too specific. Even when I try to tell myself there’s a normal explanation, I can’t really let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this belief that certain people around the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are kind of tracking me—like who I’m meeting with for clients, where I’m going, things like that. I notice little signals in invitations, panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events. Part of me tells myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go, and it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these beliefs about being tracked as something that most people would find hard to believe or understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. I know it sounds unlikely when I say it out loud. But to me it feels like there are patterns—like the invitations I get, the topics at panels, or who ends up near me at events—and it feels like they’re connected to who I’m meeting with or what I’m doing. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be coincidence, I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these beliefs about being tracked?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months, I think. That’s when I started really noticing the feeling that people were tracking my client meetings and sending those kinds of signals.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief about being tracked affected your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’m still working, but not at the same level. My practice has gotten smaller because I’ll miss follow-ups or put off reaching back out to clients. I’ve also been avoiding some public panels or events, which used to be a normal part of staying connected. So I’m managing, but it’s definitely affected how much client contact I keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about being tracked led to any significant anxiety or fear for you?\n\nPatient: I mean… some, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but it does make me tense. Like if I’m going to an event or meeting people, I start worrying about whether it’s connected somehow, and that can make me avoid it or second-guess things. It’s more a constant uneasiness than panic.\n\nDoctor: When you think about these patterns and signals, do you ever question whether they might not be real, or do you feel pretty certain that they are?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes. I’ll tell myself, you know, maybe the invitations or the panel topics or where people are sitting are just coincidences. But then it keeps lining up in a way that feels too specific, like people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I’m meeting with. So… I can doubt it for a minute, but I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the idea that people in arts and finance circles are tracking you is something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it could happen. I mean, these are real circles here in Cincinnati, and people do talk and know each other. It doesn’t feel impossible to me that someone could be keeping track of who I’m meeting with, especially through invitations or panel topics or how things are arranged at events. I know some of it could be coincidence, but it feels too specific for me to just dismiss.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that people in arts and finance circles are actually tracking you?\n\nPatient: I’d say I’m pretty certain… not 100 percent, because I do have moments where I tell myself maybe it’s coincidence. But most of the time it feels real to me. The way invitations come in, the panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events—it feels like they’re quietly tracking who I’m meeting with. I have a hard time letting that go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the people tracking you as having a logical or understandable motive for doing so?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, it feels understandable to me. In those arts and finance circles, people know each other, and who I meet with could matter to them—clients, connections, that kind of thing. So if they were trying to keep track quietly, I can see a reason for it. I know it might sound like I’m reading into things, but when the invitations and panel topics and seating keep lining up, it feels like there’s a purpose behind it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the idea of people tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements is a belief that most people would consider unusual or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. Like, if I say out loud that invitations or panel topics or where people sit near me are signals, I know that sounds unusual. But to me it still feels connected, like it keeps lining up too specifically to ignore. Even when I try to tell myself it might just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't believe people were tracking you, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: There are moments where I question it, yeah. Like I’ll tell myself maybe the invitations or the seating stuff is just coincidence. But I don’t think there’s been a real stretch of time where I fully didn’t believe it. It keeps coming back to feeling connected, and I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the belief that people are tracking you through these specific methods as something that sounds normal and reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: To me, it does feel reasonable, yeah. But in my head, the way certain invitations come up, or panel topics match things, or the same kinds of people end up near me at events… it feels too specific to just ignore. So I can tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, but I still can’t really shake the belief that they’re tracking me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your personal relationships due to this belief that people are tracking you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I still keep up with things, mostly, like work gets done in pieces. But I don’t have the same energy for people.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t call it impossible, no. I mean, people can track who meets with who, especially in finance or arts circles where everyone kind of knows each other. The part that probably sounds strange is the invitations and seating and panel topics feeling like signals.

I notice little signals in invitations, panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events. Part of me tells myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go, and it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these beliefs about being tracked as something that most people would find hard to believe or understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. I know it sounds unlikely when I say it out loud. But to me it feels like there are patterns—like the invitations I get, the topics at panels, or who ends up near me at events—and it feels like they’re connected to who I’m meeting with or what I’m doing. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be coincidence, I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these beliefs about being tracked?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months, I think. That’s when I started really noticing the feeling that people were tracking my client meetings and sending those kinds of signals.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief about being tracked affected your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’m still working, but not at the same level. My practice has gotten smaller because I’ll miss follow-ups or put off reaching back out to clients. I’ve also been avoiding some public panels or events, which used to be a normal part of staying connected. So I’m managing, but it’s definitely affected how much client contact I keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about being tracked led to any significant anxiety or fear for you?\n\nPatient: I mean… some, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but it does make me tense. Like if I’m going to an event or meeting people, I start worrying about whether it’s connected somehow, and that can make me avoid it or second-guess things. It’s more a constant uneasiness than panic.\n\nDoctor: When you think about these patterns and signals, do you ever question whether they might not be real, or do you feel pretty certain that they are?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes. I’ll tell myself, you know, maybe the invitations or the panel topics or where people are sitting are just coincidences. But then it keeps lining up in a way that feels too specific, like people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I’m meeting with. So… I can doubt it for a minute, but I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the idea that people in arts and finance circles are tracking you is something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it could happen. I mean, these are real circles here in Cincinnati, and people do talk and know each other. It doesn’t feel impossible to me that someone could be keeping track of who I’m meeting with, especially through invitations or panel topics or how things are arranged at events. I know some of it could be coincidence, but it feels too specific for me to just dismiss.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that people in arts and finance circles are actually tracking you?\n\nPatient: I’d say I’m pretty certain… not 100 percent, because I do have moments where I tell myself maybe it’s coincidence. But most of the time it feels real to me. The way invitations come in, the panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events—it feels like they’re quietly tracking who I’m meeting with. I have a hard time letting that go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the people tracking you as having a logical or understandable motive for doing so?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, it feels understandable to me. In those arts and finance circles, people know each other, and who I meet with could matter to them—clients, connections, that kind of thing. So if they were trying to keep track quietly, I can see a reason for it. I know it might sound like I’m reading into things, but when the invitations and panel topics and seating keep lining up, it feels like there’s a purpose behind it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the idea of people tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements is a belief that most people would consider unusual or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. But to me it still feels connected, like it keeps lining up too specifically to ignore. Even when I try to tell myself it might just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't believe people were tracking you, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: There are moments where I question it, yeah. Like I’ll tell myself maybe the invitations or the seating stuff is just coincidence. But I don’t think there’s been a real stretch of time where I fully didn’t believe it. It keeps coming back to feeling connected, and I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the belief that people are tracking you through these specific methods as something that sounds normal and reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: To me, it does feel reasonable, yeah. But in my head, the way certain invitations come up, or panel topics match things, or the same kinds of people end up near me at events… it feels too specific to just ignore. So I can tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, but I still can’t really shake the belief that they’re tracking me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your personal relationships due to this belief that people are tracking you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I still keep up with things, mostly, like work gets done in pieces. But I don’t have the same energy for people.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this belief that certain people around the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are kind of tracking me—like who I’m meeting with for clients, where I’m going, things like that. I notice little signals in invitations, panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events. Part of me tells myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go, and it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these beliefs about being tracked as something that most people would find hard to believe or understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. I know it sounds unlikely when I say it out loud. But to me it feels like there are patterns—like the invitations I get, the topics at panels, or who ends up near me at events—and it feels like they’re connected to who I’m meeting with or what I’m doing. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be coincidence, I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these beliefs about being tracked?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months, I think. That’s when I started really noticing the feeling that people were tracking my client meetings and sending those kinds of signals.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief about being tracked affected your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’m still working, but not at the same level. My practice has gotten smaller because I’ll miss follow-ups or put off reaching back out to clients. I’ve also been avoiding some public panels or events, which used to be a normal part of staying connected. So I’m managing, but it’s definitely affected how much client contact I keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about being tracked led to any significant anxiety or fear for you?\n\nPatient: I mean… some, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but it does make me tense. Like if I’m going to an event or meeting people, I start worrying about whether it’s connected somehow, and that can make me avoid it or second-guess things. It’s more a constant uneasiness than panic.\n\nDoctor: When you think about these patterns and signals, do you ever question whether they might not be real, or do you feel pretty certain that they are?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes. I’ll tell myself, you know, maybe the invitations or the panel topics or where people are sitting are just coincidences. But then it keeps lining up in a way that feels too specific, like people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I’m meeting with. So… I can doubt it for a minute, but I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the idea that people in arts and finance circles are tracking you is something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it could happen. I mean, these are real circles here in Cincinnati, and people do talk and know each other. It doesn’t feel impossible to me that someone could be keeping track of who I’m meeting with, especially through invitations or panel topics or how things are arranged at events. I know some of it could be coincidence, but it feels too specific for me to just dismiss.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that people in arts and finance circles are actually tracking you?\n\nPatient: I’d say I’m pretty certain… not 100 percent, because I do have moments where I tell myself maybe it’s coincidence. But most of the time it feels real to me. The way invitations come in, the panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events—it feels like they’re quietly tracking who I’m meeting with. I have a hard time letting that go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the people tracking you as having a logical or understandable motive for doing so?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, it feels understandable to me. In those arts and finance circles, people know each other, and who I meet with could matter to them—clients, connections, that kind of thing. So if they were trying to keep track quietly, I can see a reason for it. I know it might sound like I’m reading into things, but when the invitations and panel topics and seating keep lining up, it feels like there’s a purpose behind it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the idea of people tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements is a belief that most people would consider unusual or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. Like, if I say out loud that invitations or panel topics or where people sit near me are signals, I know that sounds unusual. But to me it still feels connected, like it keeps lining up too specifically to ignore. Even when I try to tell myself it might just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't believe people were tracking you, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: There are moments where I question it, yeah. Like I’ll tell myself maybe the invitations or the seating stuff is just coincidence. But I don’t think there’s been a real stretch of time where I fully didn’t believe it. It keeps coming back to feeling connected, and I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the belief that people are tracking you through these specific methods as something that sounds normal and reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: To me, it does feel reasonable, yeah. I mean, I know if I explain it to someone else, it probably sounds odd. But in my head, the way certain invitations come up, or panel topics match things, or the same kinds of people end up near me at events… it feels too specific to just ignore. So I can tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, but I still can’t really shake the belief that they’re tracking me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your personal relationships due to this belief that people are tracking you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I still keep up with things, mostly, like work gets done in pieces. But I don’t have the same energy for people. I’ll let client follow-ups sit longer than I should, and outside of work I’ve been skipping jazz nights or pickup basketball.\n\nWhen friends ask what’s going on, I usually just give short answers. It’s not that I don’t care about them exactly… talking just feels like more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the signals you think you're receiving through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements as something that could be understood or explained by others in a logical way?\n\nPatient: I think I could explain the pattern to someone, like why the invitations or panel topics feel connected to who I’m meeting with. But whether they’d see it as logical… I’m not sure. I know it probably sounds like coincidence to other people.\n\nFor me, though, it feels too specific. Even when I try to tell myself there’s a normal explanation, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of more than a few days where you didn't think people were tracking you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It’s been pretty steady for about two months now. Maybe there are moments where I’m distracted and not thinking about it, but I don’t think I’ve gone more than a few days without it coming back.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that people are tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements as bizarre or not something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t call it impossible, no. I mean, people can track who meets with who, especially in finance or arts circles where everyone kind of knows each other. The part that probably sounds strange is the invitations and seating and panel topics feeling like signals.\n\nI know that may not sound realistic to someone else, and I do tell myself it could just be coincidence. But to me it feels too connected to just brush off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of more than a week where you completely stopped believing people were tracking you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve gone a full week where I completely stopped believing it.\n\nI can have moments where I talk myself down and think, okay, maybe the invitations or seating stuff is just coincidence. But then something else happens in those arts or finance circles, and it feels connected again. So it never really goes away completely.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So I’m managing, but it’s definitely affected how much client contact I keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about being tracked led to any significant anxiety or fear for you?\n\nPatient: I mean… some, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but it does make me tense. Like if I’m going to an event or meeting people, I start worrying about whether it’s connected somehow, and that can make me avoid it or second-guess things. It’s more a constant uneasiness than panic.\n\nDoctor: When you think about these patterns and signals, do you ever question whether they might not be real, or do you feel pretty certain that they are?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes. I’ll tell myself, you know, maybe the invitations or the panel topics or where people are sitting are just coincidences. But then it keeps lining up in a way that feels too specific, like people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I’m meeting with. So… I can doubt it for a minute, but I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the idea that people in arts and finance circles are tracking you is something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it could happen. I mean, these are real circles here in Cincinnati, and people do talk and know each other. It doesn’t feel impossible to me that someone could be keeping track of who I’m meeting with, especially through invitations or panel topics or how things are arranged at events. I know some of it could be coincidence, but it feels too specific for me to just dismiss.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that people in arts and finance circles are actually tracking you?\n\nPatient: I’d say I’m pretty certain… not 100 percent, because I do have moments where I tell myself maybe it’s coincidence. But most of the time it feels real to me. The way invitations come in, the panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events—it feels like they’re quietly tracking who I’m meeting with. I have a hard time letting that go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the people tracking you as having a logical or understandable motive for doing so?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, it feels understandable to me. In those arts and finance circles, people know each other, and who I meet with could matter to them—clients, connections, that kind of thing. So if they were trying to keep track quietly, I can see a reason for it. I know it might sound like I’m reading into things, but when the invitations and panel topics and seating keep lining up, it feels like there’s a purpose behind it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the idea of people tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements is a belief that most people would consider unusual or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. Like, if I say out loud that invitations or panel topics or where people sit near me are signals, I know that sounds unusual. But to me it still feels connected, like it keeps lining up too specifically to ignore. Even when I try to tell myself it might just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't believe people were tracking you, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: There are moments where I question it, yeah. Like I’ll tell myself maybe the invitations or the seating stuff is just coincidence. But I don’t think there’s been a real stretch of time where I fully didn’t believe it. It keeps coming back to feeling connected, and I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the belief that people are tracking you through these specific methods as something that sounds normal and reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: To me, it does feel reasonable, yeah. I mean, I know if I explain it to someone else, it probably sounds odd. But in my head, the way certain invitations come up, or panel topics match things, or the same kinds of people end up near me at events… it feels too specific to just ignore. So I can tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, but I still can’t really shake the belief that they’re tracking me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your personal relationships due to this belief that people are tracking you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I still keep up with things, mostly, like work gets done in pieces. But I don’t have the same energy for people. I’ll let client follow-ups sit longer than I should, and outside of work I’ve been skipping jazz nights or pickup basketball.\n\nWhen friends ask what’s going on, I usually just give short answers. It’s not that I don’t care about them exactly… talking just feels like more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the signals you think you're receiving through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements as something that could be understood or explained by others in a logical way?\n\nPatient: I think I could explain the pattern to someone, like why the invitations or panel topics feel connected to who I’m meeting with. But whether they’d see it as logical… I’m not sure. I know it probably sounds like coincidence to other people.\n\nFor me, though, it feels too specific. Even when I try to tell myself there’s a normal explanation, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of more than a few days where you didn't think people were tracking you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It’s been pretty steady for about two months now. Maybe there are moments where I’m distracted and not thinking about it, but I don’t think I’ve gone more than a few days without it coming back.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that people are tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements as bizarre or not something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t call it impossible, no. I mean, people can track who meets with who, especially in finance or arts circles where everyone kind of knows each other. The part that probably sounds strange is the invitations and seating and panel topics feeling like signals.\n\nI know that may not sound realistic to someone else, and I do tell myself it could just be coincidence. But to me it feels too connected to just brush off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of more than a week where you completely stopped believing people were tracking you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve gone a full week where I completely stopped believing it.\n\nI can have moments where I talk myself down and think, okay, maybe the invitations or seating stuff is just coincidence. But then something else happens in those arts or finance circles, and it feels connected again. So it never really goes away completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that you might be misinterpreting these signals and that people are not actually tracking you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think there’s a possibility. I mean, I can tell myself maybe I’m reading too much into the invitations or who’s sitting near me, or the panel topics. Maybe it’s all coincidence.\n\nBut I can’t really let it go. It still feels like certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending little signals. So… I can doubt it for a minute, but I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the idea that people in arts and finance circles are tracking you is something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it could happen. I mean, these are real circles here in Cincinnati, and people do talk and know each other. It doesn’t feel impossible to me that someone could be keeping track of who I’m meeting with, especially through invitations or panel topics or how things are arranged at events. I know some of it could be coincidence, but it feels too specific for me to just dismiss.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that people in arts and finance circles are actually tracking you?\n\nPatient: I’d say I’m pretty certain… not 100 percent, because I do have moments where I tell myself maybe it’s coincidence. But most of the time it feels real to me. The way invitations come in, the panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events—it feels like they’re quietly tracking who I’m meeting with. I have a hard time letting that go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the people tracking you as having a logical or understandable motive for doing so?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, it feels understandable to me. In those arts and finance circles, people know each other, and who I meet with could matter to them—clients, connections, that kind of thing. So if they were trying to keep track quietly, I can see a reason for it. I know it might sound like I’m reading into things, but when the invitations and panel topics and seating keep lining up, it feels like there’s a purpose behind it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the idea of people tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements is a belief that most people would consider unusual or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. Like, if I say out loud that invitations or panel topics or where people sit near me are signals, I know that sounds unusual. But to me it still feels connected, like it keeps lining up too specifically to ignore. Even when I try to tell myself it might just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't believe people were tracking you, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: There are moments where I question it, yeah. Like I’ll tell myself maybe the invitations or the seating stuff is just coincidence. But I don’t think there’s been a real stretch of time where I fully didn’t believe it. It keeps coming back to feeling connected, and I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the belief that people are tracking you through these specific methods as something that sounds normal and reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: To me, it does feel reasonable, yeah. I mean, I know if I explain it to someone else, it probably sounds odd. But in my head, the way certain invitations come up, or panel topics match things, or the same kinds of people end up near me at events… it feels too specific to just ignore. So I can tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, but I still can’t really shake the belief that they’re tracking me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your personal relationships due to this belief that people are tracking you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I still keep up with things, mostly, like work gets done in pieces. But I don’t have the same energy for people. I’ll let client follow-ups sit longer than I should, and outside of work I’ve been skipping jazz nights or pickup basketball.\n\nWhen friends ask what’s going on, I usually just give short answers. It’s not that I don’t care about them exactly… talking just feels like more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the signals you think you're receiving through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements as something that could be understood or explained by others in a logical way?\n\nPatient: I think I could explain the pattern to someone, like why the invitations or panel topics feel connected to who I’m meeting with. Even when I try to tell myself there’s a normal explanation, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of more than a few days where you didn't think people were tracking you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It’s been pretty steady for about two months now. Maybe there are moments where I’m distracted and not thinking about it, but I don’t think I’ve gone more than a few days without it coming back.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that people are tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements as bizarre or not something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t call it impossible, no. I mean, people can track who meets with who, especially in finance or arts circles where everyone kind of knows each other. The part that probably sounds strange is the invitations and seating and panel topics feeling like signals.\n\nI know that may not sound realistic to someone else, and I do tell myself it could just be coincidence. But to me it feels too connected to just brush off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of more than a week where you completely stopped believing people were tracking you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve gone a full week where I completely stopped believing it.\n\nI can have moments where I talk myself down and think, okay, maybe the invitations or seating stuff is just coincidence. But then something else happens in those arts or finance circles, and it feels connected again. But then it keeps lining up in a way that feels too specific, like people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I’m meeting with. So… I can doubt it for a minute, but I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the idea that people in arts and finance circles are tracking you is something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it could happen. I mean, these are real circles here in Cincinnati, and people do talk and know each other. It doesn’t feel impossible to me that someone could be keeping track of who I’m meeting with, especially through invitations or panel topics or how things are arranged at events. I know some of it could be coincidence, but it feels too specific for me to just dismiss.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that people in arts and finance circles are actually tracking you?\n\nPatient: I’d say I’m pretty certain… not 100 percent, because I do have moments where I tell myself maybe it’s coincidence. But most of the time it feels real to me. The way invitations come in, the panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events—it feels like they’re quietly tracking who I’m meeting with. I have a hard time letting that go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the people tracking you as having a logical or understandable motive for doing so?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, it feels understandable to me. In those arts and finance circles, people know each other, and who I meet with could matter to them—clients, connections, that kind of thing. So if they were trying to keep track quietly, I can see a reason for it. I know it might sound like I’m reading into things, but when the invitations and panel topics and seating keep lining up, it feels like there’s a purpose behind it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the idea of people tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements is a belief that most people would consider unusual or hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. Like, if I say out loud that invitations or panel topics or where people sit near me are signals, I know that sounds unusual. But to me it still feels connected, like it keeps lining up too specifically to ignore. Even when I try to tell myself it might just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you didn't believe people were tracking you, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: There are moments where I question it, yeah. Like I’ll tell myself maybe the invitations or the seating stuff is just coincidence. But I don’t think there’s been a real stretch of time where I fully didn’t believe it. It keeps coming back to feeling connected, and I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the belief that people are tracking you through these specific methods as something that sounds normal and reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: To me, it does feel reasonable, yeah. I mean, I know if I explain it to someone else, it probably sounds odd. But in my head, the way certain invitations come up, or panel topics match things, or the same kinds of people end up near me at events… it feels too specific to just ignore. So I can tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, but I still can’t really shake the belief that they’re tracking me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your personal relationships due to this belief that people are tracking you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I still keep up with things, mostly, like work gets done in pieces. But I don’t have the same energy for people. I’ll let client follow-ups sit longer than I should, and outside of work I’ve been skipping jazz nights or pickup basketball.\n\nWhen friends ask what’s going on, I usually just give short answers. It’s not that I don’t care about them exactly… talking just feels like more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the signals you think you're receiving through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements as something that could be understood or explained by others in a logical way?\n\nPatient: I think I could explain the pattern to someone, like why the invitations or panel topics feel connected to who I’m meeting with. But whether they’d see it as logical… I’m not sure. I know it probably sounds like coincidence to other people.\n\nFor me, though, it feels too specific. Even when I try to tell myself there’s a normal explanation, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of more than a few days where you didn't think people were tracking you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It’s been pretty steady for about two months now. Maybe there are moments where I’m distracted and not thinking about it, but I don’t think I’ve gone more than a few days without it coming back.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that people are tracking you through invitations, panel topics, and seating arrangements as bizarre or not something that could happen in real life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t call it impossible, no. I mean, people can track who meets with who, especially in finance or arts circles where everyone kind of knows each other. The part that probably sounds strange is the invitations and seating and panel topics feeling like signals.\n\nI know that may not sound realistic to someone else, and I do tell myself it could just be coincidence. But to me it feels too connected to just brush off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of more than a week where you completely stopped believing people were tracking you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve gone a full week where I completely stopped believing it.\n\nI can have moments where I talk myself down and think, okay, maybe the invitations or seating stuff is just coincidence. But then something else happens in those arts or finance circles, and it feels connected again. So it never really goes away completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that you might be misinterpreting these signals and that people are not actually tracking you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do think there’s a possibility. I mean, I can tell myself maybe I’m reading too much into the invitations or who’s sitting near me, or the panel topics. Maybe it’s all coincidence.\n\nBut I can’t really let it go. It still feels like certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending little signals. So I know I might be misinterpreting it, but it doesn’t feel that way most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would agree with your belief that you're being tracked?\n\nPatient: Probably not most people, no. I think a lot of people would say I’m reading too much into it, like the invitations or panel topics or where people sit are just normal event stuff.\n\nBut to me it still feels connected, especially with certain people in the arts and finance circles here. So I know others might not agree, but I still have a hard time dismissing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the signals you believe you're receiving could be explained by anything other than people tracking you?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, it could be. I can see how invitations or panel topics or where people sit near me at events could just be coincidence or normal networking stuff.\n\nBut when it keeps happening around the same arts and finance people here in Cincinnati, and it lines up with clients I’ve met with, it’s hard for me to let that explanation stick. Part of me knows there might be another reason, but it still feels like they’re tracking me somehow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
